Monadic and Comonadic Aspects of Dependency Analysis Journal: PACMPL CCS: Security and privacy Formal security models CCS: Theory of computation Type theory CCS: Theory of computation Categorical semantics
Pritam Choudhury

Abstract
. Dependency analysis is vital to several applications in computer science. It lies at the essence of secure information flow analysis, binding-time analysis, etc. Various calculi have been proposed in the literature for analysing individual dependencies. Abadi et. al., by extending Moggi’s monadic metalanguage, unified several of these calculi into the Dependency Core Calculus (DCC). DCC has served as a foundational framework for dependency analysis for the last two decades. However, in spite of its success, DCC has its limitations. First, the monadic bind rule of the calculus is nonstandard and relies upon an auxiliary protection judgement. Second, being of a monadic nature, the calculus cannot capture dependency analyses that possess a comonadic nature, for example, the binding-time calculus, λ ∘ \lambda^{\circ} , of Davies. In this paper, we address these limitations by designing an alternative dependency calculus that is inspired by standard ideas from category theory. Our calculus is both monadic and comonadic in nature and subsumes both DCC and λ ∘ \lambda^{\circ} . Our construction explains the nonstandard bind rule and the protection judgement of DCC in terms of standard categorical concepts. It also leads to a novel technique for proving correctness of dependency analysis. We use this technique to present alternative proofs of correctness for DCC and λ ∘ \lambda^{\circ} .



1.
Introduction

Dependency analysis is the analysis of dependence of an entity upon another. The entities are primarily programs or parts thereof, but they can also be abstract, like security clearance levels in an organization, stages in a compilation process, etc.

Broadly speaking, an entity depends upon another one if the latter influences the behaviour of the former. On the other hand, an entity is independent of another one if the latter does not interfere in the behaviour of the former. For example, consider the following
\lambda
-terms:
(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\>(2+2)
and
(\lambda\mathit{x}.4)\>(2+2)
. The argument
(2+2)
dictates the normal form of the first term whereas it plays no role in deciding the normal form of the second term. So, we say that the first term depends upon the argument whereas the second one does not. What this means is that in the second term, we can replace the argument
(2+2)
with any other terminating computation, while maintaining the same normal form for the term as a whole.

The power of dependency analysis comes from this very simple principle: if an entity does not depend upon another one, then variations in the latter should not affect the former. This is the well-known principle of noninterference
(
Goguen and
Meseguer 1982
)
. This principle has far-reaching implications and lies at the heart of several applications in computer science, like secure information flow analysis, binding-time analysis, etc.

In secure information flow analysis
(
Denning 1976
;
Denning and
Denning 1977
;
Smith 2007
)
, one wishes to guarantee that there is no flow of information from secret data to public variables. Viewed abstractly in terms of security levels, this is equivalent to saying that level ‘public’
does not depend
upon level ‘secret’. In binding-time analysis
(
Hatcliff and
Danvy 1997
;
Gomard and Jones 1991
;
Glück and
Jørgensen 1995
;
Davies 2017
)
, one wishes to guarantee that a given program can be correctly compiled in multiple stages even when each stage can potentially optimize based on inputs received from earlier stages. To ensure correctness of such compilation, it is necessary that an earlier stage
does not depend
upon a later one. Secure information flow analysis and binding-time analysis are examples of dependency analysis. There are many other examples of dependency analysis
(
Palsberg and
Ørbæk 1995
;
Tofte and Talpin 1997
;
Tip 1995
;
Tang and Jouvelot 1995
, etc.)
appearing in the literature.

Over two decades ago,
Abadi
et al. 1999
showed that several dependency analyses
(
Heintze and
Riecke 1998
;
Tip 1995
;
Hatcliff and
Danvy 1997
;
Volpano
et al. 1996
;
Tang and Jouvelot 1995
)
can be seen as instances of a general Dependency Core Calculus (DCC). Their work has served as a foundational framework for dependency analysis in the field of programming languages and has led to extensive research
(
Tse and Zdancewic 2004
;
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
;
Bowman and Ahmed 2015
;
Algehed and
Bernardy 2019
;
Algehed 2018
;
Choudhury
et al. 2022
, etc.)
on this topic which continues to this day.

DCC is a simple extension of Moggi’s monadic metalanguage
(
Moggi 1991
)
. The monadic metalanguage is a general calculus for analysing computational effects like nontermination, exceptions, input/output, etc. Moggi showed that computational effects in programming languages can be understood in terms of monads from category theory
(
MacLane 1971
)
. At first sight, computational effects seem to be quite different from dependencies. So, it comes as a surprise (pointed out by
Abadi
et al. 1999
themselves) that with just a simple extension, a calculus for analysing computational effects can also analyse dependencies.

In this regard,
Abadi
et al. 1999
point out a common feature underlying monads and security levels: just as there is no way of projecting out of a ‘monad world’, there is also no way of projecting out of a ‘secret world’. Concretely, just as there is no general function of type
T\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
for a monad
T
and a type
\mathit{A}
, there is also no non-trivial function from secret data to public variables. So, dependency analysis has a monadic aspect to it.

However, the monadic aspect of dependency analysis might be just half of the story. Everyday experience shows us that security constraints can be enforced not only by restricting outflow but also by restricting inflow. For example, in a world with two levels,
\mathbf{Public}
and
\mathbf{Secret}
, security can be enforced not only by restricting projection out of
\mathbf{Secret}
level, but also by restricting injection into
\mathbf{Public}
level. These ways are dual to one another. The way of restricting projection, employed in DCC, goes via monads. On the other hand, the way of restricting injection goes via comonads. Similar to a monad
T
that restricts by disallowing a general function of type
T\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
, a comonad
D
restricts by disallowing a general function of type
\mathit{A}\to D\mathit{A}
. While the monadic aspect of dependency analysis has received considerable attention in literature, the comonadic aspect has received less attention.

In this paper, we show that just like the monadic aspect, the comonadic aspect of dependency analysis also has much to offer. Further, we find that the monadic and the comonadic aspects play nicely with one another. We design a language that integrates these two aspects into a single system. This integration helps us unify DCC, a monadic dependency calculus and
\lambda^{\circ}
(
Davies 2017
)
, a comonadic dependency calculus (that is known to be outside the reach of DCC
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
)
). It also leads to a novel general technique for proving correctness of dependency analysis. And above all, it shines light on some of the nuances of dependency analysis.

In short, we make the following contributions:

•

We present a Graded Monadic Comonadic Calculus, GMCC, and its extension,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, and provide meaning-preserving translations from both DCC and
\lambda^{\circ}
to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
.

•

We show that the protection judgement of DCC, when appropriately modified, enables comonadic reasoning in the language. Further, we show that under certain restrictions, DCC, with this modification, is equivalent to GMCC. This equivalence helps explain the nonstandard bind-rule of DCC in terms of standard categorical concepts.

•

GMCC and
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
are general calculi that are sound with respect to a class of categorical models. These categorical models motivate a novel technique for proving correctness of dependency analyses. We use this technique to provide simple proofs of correctness for both DCC and
\lambda^{\circ}
.

Note that this paper is an extended version of
Choudhury 2022
. The reader can find the proofs of the lemmas and theorems stated in
Choudhury 2022
in the appendices of this paper.

In the next section, we review the basics of dependency analysis and its application in information flow control and staged execution of programs. This section is meant to provide some background to readers who are not very familiar with dependency analysis.

2.
Dependency Analysis in Action

Consider a database
Db
containing demographic information of a city. For the sake of simplicity, let’s say the database is represented as a list of tuples with elements of the list corresponding to residents of the city and elements of the tuples corresponding to their demographic information. Further, let’s assume that each tuple has only 4 elements for recording name, age, ethnicity and monthly income of a resident (in that order). According to the policies of the city council, name, age and ethnicities of the residents are non-sensitive information that may be shared without any constraint, whereas monthly income of the residents is sensitive information that may be shared only with people who are allowed to handle such information.

Now, consider the following queries:

(1)

What fraction of the elderly city residents (age
\geq
65 years) are ethnically Caucasian?

(2)

What is the average monthly income of ethnically Asian residents of the city?

With the above queries in mind, a programmer seeks the outputs of the following programs, written in
Haskell
-like syntax:

(1)

⬇

lstEld

=

filter

(\

x

->

second

x

>=

65)

Db

lstEldC

=

filter

(\

x

->

third

x

==

"
Caucasian
")

lstEld

fracEldC

=

(
length

lstEldC
)

/

(
length

lstEld
)

print

fracEldC

(2)

⬇

lstAsn

=

filter

(\

x

->

third

x

==

"
Asian
")

Db

totIncA

=

foldr

(\

x

y

->

fourth x

+

y
)

0

lstAsn

avgIncA

=

totIncA

/

(
length

lstAsn
)

print

avgIncA

Note that functions
second
,
third
and
fourth
access the second, third and fourth elements of a tuple respectively. The question we need to address now is whether the programmer may be allowed to see the outputs of the above programs. Let’s suppose this programmer does not have the permission to handle sensitive information. Then, the output of the second program should not be shared with this programmer because it reveals (at least partially) monthly income data. For example, if there’s just a single ethnically Asian resident in the city, then the output of the second program gives away the monthly income of that resident. The output of the first program may, however, be shared freely with this programmer because it does not reveal any sensitive information.

Next, how do we reach this conclusion about sharing output by
just
analysing the respective programs? In other words, given a program, how do we decide whether or not its output reveals any sensitive information? To answer this question, we need to perform a dependency analysis called information flow analysis: If the output of a program
depends upon
any information deemed sensitive, then the output too needs to be treated as sensitive. Conversely, if the output
does not depend upon
any sensitive information, then the output too is not sensitive. In the second program above, the output,
avgIncA
, is sensitive because it
depends upon

totIncA
, which in turn
depends upon

fourth

x
, a sensitive piece of information. On the other hand, in the first program, all the data used to compute the output are non-sensitive, rendering the output itself non-sensitive.

To analyse dependency of output upon sensitive information, information flow calculi typically incorporate sensitivity of information in the types themselves. For example, the type of
Db
would be
[(
T

L

String
,

T

L

Int
,

T

L

String
,

T

H

Int
)]
, where
T

l

String
and
T

l

Int
are the types of
l
-security strings and integers respectively, with
l
being
H
or
L
, corresponding to high-security and low-security respectively. In information flow calculi, functions, too, have types that take sensitivity of information into account. For example, the type of ‘
+
’ would be
T

l

Int

->

T

l

Int

->

T

l

Int
. Now, if we write the above programs in such a calculus, we would see that
fracEldC
has type
T L Double
whereas
avgIncA
has type
T H Double
. Any user can access terms of type
T L Double
but terms of type
T H Double
are only accessible to users with high-security clearance. Within the calculus, the noninterference property enforces this restriction. In this way, information flow calculi ensure secure flow of information.

Our next example takes up another form of dependency analysis: binding-time analysis. Binding-time analysis helps in staged execution of programs. Staged execution comes in handy when programs have inputs that are statically known in addition to inputs that are known only at run-time. Computations that depend only upon static inputs may be carried out statically, thereby producing residual programs that can be executed faster at run-time. To find out which computations depend only upon static inputs, we perform a dependency analysis, called binding-time analysis.

Next, we shall use the same database example to show binding-time analysis in action. However, instead of sensitivity of information, here we focus on availability of information. From the given demographic parameters, name and ethnicity remain constant over time whereas monthly income varies. To account for this fact, the city council mandates that every resident update their monthly income on the last day of each month. Now, to get an accurate answer to the second query, one needs to run the second program on such days. But if the database contains millions of entries, running this program may burden the computing system on these days. However, observe that some or perhaps most of the work done by this program need not wait for the update in monthly incomes. For example,
lstAsn
can be computed statically beforehand because this computation depends only upon static information. Binding-time analysis identifies such computations, thereby enabling faster execution of programs.

Binding-time calculi typically incorporate information about binding-time in the types themselves. For example, the type of
Db
would be
[(
T

Sta

String
,

T

Sta

Int
,

T

Sta

String
,

T

Dyn

Int
)]
, where
T

l

String
and
T

l

Int
are respectively the types of strings and integers available at time
l
, with
l
being
Dyn
or
Sta
, corresponding to dynamic availability and static availability respectively. In binding-time calculi, functions, too, have types that take binding-time information into account. For example, the type of ‘
+
’ would be
T

l

Int

->

T

l

Int

->

T

l

Int
. If we write the second program in such a calculus, we would see that the computation of
lstAsn
depends upon static data only (To compute
totIncA
, however, we would need dynamic data,
fourth

x
). So we could compute
lstAsn
statically and thereafter run
only the residual program
on the last day of every month, thereby reducing the burden on the computing system on such days. To give an estimate, if ethnically Asian residents constitute 5% of the total population (say), then, compared to the original program, the residual program may run 20x faster.

With this background on dependency analysis, we shall now work towards building our dependency calculi. We shall present two key dependency calculi in this paper: GMCC and its extension,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
. The calculus GMCC is built up from a graded monadic calculus, GMC, and a graded comonadic calculus, GCC. In the next section, we look at the Graded Monadic Calculus (GMC).

3.
Graded Monadic Calculus

Moggi
(
Moggi 1991
)
showed that computational effects can be understood in terms of monads. On the other hand,
Gifford and
Lucassen 1986
showed that side effects can be tracked using effect classes.
Wadler and
Thiemann 2003
later adapted effect classes to monads but left open the question of a general theory of effects and monads. Eventually,
Katsumata 2014
presented such a general theory in terms of graded monads. In this section, we adapt Katsumata’s Explicit Subeffecting Calculus to present a simply-typed Graded Monadic Calculus (GMC).

GMC is an extension of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with a grade-annotated monadic type constructor
T_{m}
. The grades,
m
, are drawn from an arbitrary preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}=(M,\_\cdot\_,1,\leq)
. Recall that a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
is a monoid
(M,\_\cdot\_,1)
along with a preorder
\leq
such that the order respects the binary operator, meaning if
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, then
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. Note that whenever we need to be precise, we use GMC(
\mathcal{M}
) to refer to GMC parametrized by
\mathcal{M}
. We follow the same convention for other calculi parametrized by algebraic structures.

Next, we present the calculus formally.

3.1.
Grammar and Type System

The grammar of the calculus appears in Figure
1
. In addition to the types and terms of standard
\lambda
-calculus, we have a graded monadic type
T_{m}\>\mathit{A}
and terms related to it. The typing rules of the calculus appear in Figure
2
. We omit the typing rules of standard
\lambda
-calculus and consider only the ones related to the graded monadic type.

\displaystyle\text{types},A,B

\displaystyle::=\mathbf{Unit}\>|\>\mathbf{Void}\>|\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\>|\>\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}\>|\>\mathit{A}+\mathit{B}\>|\>T_{m}\>\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\text{terms},a,b,f,g

\displaystyle::=\mathit{x}\>|\>\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\>|\>\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\>|\>(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\>|\>\mathbf{proj}_{1}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{proj}_{2}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{unit}

\displaystyle\>|\>\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{abort}\>\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\>|\>\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\>|\>\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\text{contexts},\Gamma

\displaystyle::=\emptyset\>|\>\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}

.
Grammar of GMC

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}

(Typing rules)

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}:T_{1}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:M-Return}{{{M-Return}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:M-Fmap}{{{M-Fmap}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:M-Join}{{{M-Join}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}\quad m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:M-Up}{{{M-Up}}}
}{},}

Figure 2
.
Typing rules of GMC (excerpt)

The rules
M-Return
,
M-Fmap
, and
M-Join
are generalizations of the corresponding rules for the ungraded monadic type. Note that the rule
M-Join
‘joins’ the grades using the binary operator of the monoid. The rule
M-Up
relaxes the grade on the monadic type. If
\mathcal{M}
is the trivial preordered monoid, then the above rules degenerate to the standard typing rules for monads.

Next, we look at the equational theory of the calculus.

3.2.
Equational Theory

Equality over terms of the graded monadic calculus is a congruent equivalence relation generated by the standard
\beta\eta
-equality rules over
\lambda
-terms and the additional rules that appear in Figure
3
. For presenting the rules, we use the shorthand notation:
\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{f}\triangleq\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}((\mathbf{lift}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{a})
where
a:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
and
f:\mathit{A}\to T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{B}
. Note that
\_\!\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\_
is a graded
\mathbf{bind}
-operator.

(1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x}))

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.(\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g})\>((\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{x})

(3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}

(4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\mathit{a}

(5)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a})\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{f}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{f})

(6)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{b})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{b})

(7)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a})\>\mathchoice{\kern 4.48613pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 4.48613pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 7.47655pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 8.94144pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m}\mathit{f}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a}

(8)

\displaystyle\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{1}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{x})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}

(9)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt(\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{f})\>\mathchoice{\kern 24.8729pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 24.8729pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 26.45134pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 27.91623pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\mathit{g}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}(\lambda\mathit{x}.(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\gg}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\gg\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\mathit{g}))

Figure 3
.
Equality rules of GMC (excerpt)

The first two rules correspond to preservation of identity function and composition of functions by
\mathbf{lift}
. The next two rules correspond to reflexivity and transitivity of the order relation. The two rules after that correspond to commutativity of
\mathbf{bind}
with
\mathbf{up}
. The two subsequent rules correspond to
\mathbf{ret}
being the left and the right identity of
\mathbf{bind}
. The last rule corresponds to associativity of
\mathbf{bind}
.

We now want to interpret this calculus in a suitable category. The types of standard
\lambda
-calculus can be interpreted in any bicartesian closed category. To interpret the graded monadic type, we need a graded monad.
Fujii 2019
provides a nice account on graded monads and graded comonads. However, for the sake of self-containment, we shall briefly review the theory behind graded monads in the following section.

3.3.
Graded Monads

A graded monad is a certain kind of lax monoidal functor
(
MacLane 1971
)
. A lax monoidal functor from a monoidal category
(M,\otimes_{M},1_{M})
to a monoidal category
(N,\otimes_{N},1_{N})
is a 3-tuple
(F,F_{2},F_{0})
where,

•

F
is a functor from
M
to
N

•

For
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(M)
, morphisms

F_{2}(X,Y):F(X)\otimes_{N}F(Y)\to F(X\otimes_{M}Y)
are natural in
X
and
Y

•

F_{0}:1_{N}\to F(1_{M})

such that the diagrams in Figure
4
commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(1_{M})\otimes_{N}F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)\otimes_{N}F(1_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(1_{M},X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}\otimes_{N}\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes_{N}F_{0}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(X,1_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)\otimes_{N}F(Y)\otimes_{N}F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)\otimes_{N}F(Y\otimes_{M}Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes_{M}Y)\otimes_{N}F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes_{M}Y\otimes_{M}Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes_{N}F_{2}(Y,Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(X,Y)\otimes_{N}\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(X,Y\otimes_{M}Z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}(X\otimes_{M}Y,Z)}

Figure 4
.
Commutative diagrams for lax monoidal functor

Note that here we assume
M
and
N
to be strict monoidal categories, i.e.
1_{M}\otimes_{M}X=X=X\otimes 1_{M}
and
(X\otimes_{M}Y)\otimes_{M}Z=X\otimes_{M}(Y\otimes_{M}Z)
for any
X,Y,Z\in\text{Obj}(M)
, and similarly for
N
.

An example of a strict monoidal category is a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
. Any preorder
(M,\leq)
may be seen as a category,
\mathbf{C}({(M,\leq)})
, that has
M
as its set of objects and a unique morphism from
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}
to
m_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if and only if
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. Identity morphisms and composition of morphisms are given by reflexivity and transitivity of the order relation. Now, a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}=(M,\_\cdot\_,1,\leq)
may be seen as a strict monoidal category:
\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{M}})=(\mathbf{C}({(M,\leq)}),\_\cdot\_,1)
.

Another example of a strict monoidal category is the category of endofunctors. Given any category
\mathds{C}
, the endofunctors of
\mathds{C}
form a strict monoidal category,
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}
, with the tensor product given by composition
\_\circ\_
of functors and the identity object given by the identity functor
Id
.

We use these two monoidal categories to define a graded monad. An
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad over
\mathds{C}
is a lax monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{M})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}
. We wish to use an
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad to interpret GMC(
\mathcal{M}
). However, such a monad doesn’t stand up to the task (Try interpreting rule
M-Fmap
!). This shortcoming should not come as a surprise because we know that monads, in and of themselves, cannot model the monadic type constructor of Moggi’s computational metalanguage
(
Moggi 1991
)
. For that, they need to be accompanied with tensorial strengths. Here too, we need to add tensorial strengths to graded monads to interpret the graded monadic type constructor. One could define tensorial strength separately after having defined a graded monad first. However, in lieu, one can also just define a strong graded monad in one go using the category of strong endofunctors and strong natural transformations.

An endofunctor
F
on a monoidal category
(M,\otimes,1,\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
is said to be strong
(
Eilenberg and
Kelly 1966
;
Kock 1970
;
Kock 1972
)
if there exists morphisms
t_{X,Y}:X\otimes F(Y)\to F(X\otimes Y)
, natural in
X
and
Y
, for
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(M)
such that the diagrams in Figure
5
commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes Y)\otimes FZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F((X\otimes Y)\otimes Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes(Y\otimes FZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes F(Y\otimes Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes(Y\otimes Z))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{X\otimes Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{X,Y,FZ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\alpha^{-1}_{X,Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes t_{Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{X,Y\otimes Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(1\otimes X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{FX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda_{X}}

Figure 5
.
Commutative diagrams for strong endofunctor

Given strong endofunctors
(F,t^{F})
and
(G,t^{G})
, a natural transformation
\alpha:F\to G
is said to be strong, if for any
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(M
), the diagram in Figure
6
commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes FY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes GY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\otimes Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(X\otimes Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{F}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\alpha_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{G}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X\otimes Y}}

Figure 6
.
Commutative diagram for strong natural transformation

Strong endofunctors can be defined for arbitrary monoidal categories; however, we just need the ones over cartesian monoidal categories. Given any cartesian category
\mathds{C}
, let
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
denote the category with objects: strong endofunctors over
(\mathds{C},\times,\top)
(where
\top
is the terminal object) and morphisms: strong natural transformations between them. Like
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}
, category
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
too is strict monoidal with the monoidal product and the identity object defined in the same way.

Finally, we have the definition of a strong graded monad. Given a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
and a cartesian category
\mathds{C}
, a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad over
\mathds{C}
is a lax monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
. Using strong graded monads, we can now provide a categorical model for the graded monadic calculus.

3.4.
Categorical Model

Let
\mathds{C}
be any bicartesian closed category. Let
(\mathbf{T},\mu,\eta)
be a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad over
\mathds{C}
. Then, the interpretation,
\llbracket\_\rrbracket
, of types and terms is as follows: The types and terms of standard
\lambda
-calculus are interpreted in the usual way. The graded monadic type and terms related to it are interpreted in Figure
7
.

\displaystyle\llbracket T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\eta\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mu^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

Figure 7
.
Interpretation of GMC (excerpt)

There are a few things to note here:

•

\mathbf{T}(m)
, written as
\mathbf{T}_{m}
, is a functor

•

\mathbf{T}({m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
, written as
\mathbf{T}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, is a natural transformation

•

\eta
is a natural transformation from
Id
to
\mathbf{T}_{1}

•

\mu^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are morphisms from
\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\circ\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
to
\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, and are natural in both
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
m_{{\mathrm{2}}}

•

t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}
denotes the strength of
\mathbf{T}_{m}

•

\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{-1}
denote currying and uncurrying respectively

Let us now see why this interpretation satisfies the equational theory of the calculus. Equations (
1
) and (
2
) follow because
\mathbf{T}_{m}
is a functor, for any
m\in M
. Equations (
3
) and (
4
) follow because
\mathbf{T}
is a functor and as such, preserves identity morphisms and composition of morphisms. Equations (
5
) and (
6
) follow because
\mu
is natural in its first component and its second component respectively. Equations (
7
) and (
8
) follow respectively from the left and the right unit laws for graded monad, laws that correspond to the commutative triangles in Figure
4
. Equation (
9
) follows from the associative law for graded monad, the law that corresponds to the commutative square in Figure
4
. We shall point out that soundness of the equations in the model also depends upon the axioms about strength, shown in Figure
5
.

Thus, a bicartesian closed category
\mathds{C}
with
(\mathbf{T},\mu,\eta)
, a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad over
\mathds{C}
, is a sound model for GMC(
\mathcal{M}
).

Theorem 3.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

4.
DCC and GMC

In this section, we look at the relation between DCC and GMC. Since DCC lies at the heart of our paper, we next review the calculus briefly. For simplicity, we first focus on the terminating fragment of the calculus and consider non-termination later in our paper.

4.1.
Dependency Core Calculus

The Dependency Core Calculus is simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus, extended with multiple type constructors,
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
, which help analyse dependencies. The indices,
\ell
, are elements of an abstract lattice
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\wedge,\bot,\top)
. The lattice structure for the calculus is motivated by the lattice model of secure information flow
(
Denning 1976
)
. The elements of a lattice model may be thought of as dependency levels, with
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
meaning
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
may depend upon
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\neg(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
meaning
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
should not depend upon
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
. (Here,
\sqsubseteq
is the implied order of the lattice.) For example, public and secret levels may be modelled using a two-point lattice
\mathcal{L}_{2}
:
\mathbf{Public}\sqsubset\mathbf{Secret}
.

DCC uses an auxiliary protection judgement to analyse dependency. The protection judgement, written
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, and presented in Figure
8
, can be read as: the terms of type
\mathit{A}
may depend upon level
\ell
. Another way to read it is: the terms of type
\mathit{A}
are at least as secure as level
\ell
. With this reading of the protection judgement, the calculus may be said to be correct when it satisfies the following condition: if
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
and
\neg(\ell\sqsubseteq\ell^{\prime})
, then the terms of
\mathit{A}
are not be visible at
\ell^{\prime}
. Let us now look at the type system and equational theory of DCC.

\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}

(DCC Protect)

\frac{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\quad\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}}{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\times\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Prod}{{{Prot-Prod}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}}{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Fun}{{{Prot-Fun}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Monad}{{{Prot-Monad}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}}{\ell\sqsubseteq T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Already}{{{Prot-Already}}}
}{},}

Figure 8
.
Protection rules for DCC

4.2.
Type System and Equational Theory of DCC

The typing rules of DCC consist of the ones for standard
\lambda
-calculus along with the introduction and elimination rules for
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
, shown below.

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}

(DCC Typing (Excerpt))

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{a}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:DCC-Eta}{{{DCC-Eta}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathit{B}\quad\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:DCC-Bind}{{{DCC-Bind}}}
}{},}

The protection judgement in rule
DCC-Bind
ensures that
\mathit{a}
is visible to
\mathit{B}
only if
\mathit{B}
has the necessary permission. Note that rule
DCC-Bind
, unlike a standard monadic bind rule, does not wrap the return type,
\mathit{B}
, with the constructor
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
. This difference is significant and we shall see its implications as we go along.

Now we consider the equational theory of DCC.
Abadi
et al. 1999
do not explicitly provide an equational theory for DCC. However, they provide an operational semantics for DCC. We describe the equational theory corresponding to the operational semantics they provide. The terms of DCC can be seen as
\lambda
-terms annotated with security labels. If we erase the annotations, we are left with plain
\lambda
-terms. Plain
\lambda
-terms already have an equational theory: the one generated by the standard
\beta\eta
-rules. Using this theory, we define the equational theory of DCC as follows: two DCC terms are equal, if and only if, after erasure, they are equal as
\lambda
-terms, i.e.
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\simeq\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\triangleq\lfloor\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rfloor\equiv\lfloor\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rfloor
, where
\lfloor\mathit{a}\rfloor
is the plain
\lambda
-term corresponding to the DCC-term
\mathit{a}
.

Now we are in a position to explore the relation between DCC and GMC. There are two questions that we would like to address.

•

Is DCC a graded monadic calculus? In other words, with appropriate restrictions, can we translate GMC to DCC while preserving meaning?

•

Is DCC just a graded monadic calculus? In other words, with appropriate restrictions, can we translate DCC to GMC while preserving meaning?

We shall see that the answer to the first question is yes, while the answer to the second one is no.

4.3.
Is DCC a Graded Monadic Calculus?

Both DCC and GMC are calculi parametrized by algebraic structures. To compare the calculi, we first need to relate the parametrizing structures. DCC is parametrized by an arbitrary lattice
\mathcal{L}
whereas GMC is parametrized by an arbitrary preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
. A preordered monoid is a more general structure because any bounded semilattice may be seen as a preordered monoid. For example, a bounded join-semilattice is a preordered monoid with multiplication, unit and the preorder given by join,
\bot
and the semilattice order respectively. A point to note here is that in the original formulation of
Denning 1976
, the semantics of secure information flow just constrains the model to be a bounded join-semilattice. However, under the practical assumption of finiteness, such a model collapses to a lattice. Here, we shall compare DCC and GMC over the class of bounded join-semilattices.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\bot)
be a bounded join-semilattice. Then, the translation,
\overline{\phantom{a}}
, from GMC to DCC, is given in Figure
9
. This translation preserves typing and meaning.

Theorem 4.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\Gamma}\vdash\overline{\mathit{a}}:\overline{\mathit{A}}
in DCC(
\mathcal{L}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\simeq\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in DCC(
\mathcal{L}
).

\displaystyle\overline{T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\overline{\mathit{A}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{eta}^{\bot}\overline{\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{lift}^{\ell}\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\overline{\mathit{A}}.\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}(\overline{\mathit{f}}\>\mathit{y})\hskip 3.0pt[\text{Here, }\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}]

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{join}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{x}=\overline{\mathit{a}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{y}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{up}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{x}=\overline{\mathit{a}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{x}

Figure 9
.
Translation function from GMC to DCC (excerpt)

4.4.
Is DCC Just a Graded Monadic Calculus?

Now that GMC can be translated into DCC, can we go the other way around? Let’s explore this question. To translate DCC to GMC, we would need to translate the
\mathbf{bind}
construct. We may attempt a translation for
\mathbf{bind}
of DCC using
\mathbf{bind}
of GMC. However, note that the signature of
\mathbf{bind}
in GMC is:
T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to(\mathit{A}\to T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{B})\to T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{B}
whereas that of
\mathbf{bind}
in DCC is:
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})\to\{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}\}\to B
. For a successful translation, one needs to show that, if
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
, then there exists a function
\mathit{j}
of type
(T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to\underline{\mathit{B}})
. In case such a function exists, for
\mathit{a}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
and
\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
, one can get
(\mathit{j}\>((\mathbf{lift}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\underline{\mathit{f}})\>\underline{\mathit{a}})):\underline{\mathit{B}}
. (Here,
\underline{\phantom{a}}
denotes a possible translation of DCC to GMC.)

We attempt to define
\mathit{j}:T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to\underline{\mathit{B}}
via structural recursion on the judgement
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
. The interesting cases are rules
Prot-Monad
and
Prot-Already
.

•

Rule
Prot-Monad
. Here, we have
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathcal{T}_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\mathit{B}
where
\ell\sqsubseteq\ell^{\prime}
. Need to define
\mathit{j}:T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}
. But,
\mathit{x}:T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\vdash\mathbf{join}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\mathit{x}:T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}
because
\ell\vee\ell^{\prime}=\ell^{\prime}
.

•

Rule
Prot-Already
. Here, we have
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathcal{T}_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\mathit{B}
where
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
. Need to define
\mathit{j}:T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}
. Since
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
, the hypothesis gives us a function
\mathit{j_{{\mathrm{0}}}}:T_{\ell}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to\underline{\mathit{B}}
. But, now we are stuck! Lifting this function can only give us:
\mathbf{lift}^{\ell^{\prime}}\mathit{j_{{\mathrm{0}}}}:T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>T_{\ell}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to T_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}
, not exactly what we need.

Here, we could, for instance, add a non-standard flip-rule to GMC like: “from
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, derive
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{flip}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
” and thereafter translate DCC into it. But such an exercise would defeat our whole purpose because then, GMC would no longer be a graded monadic calculus. Note that
Algehed 2018
includes such a rule in his language SDCC, which is shown to be equivalent to (the terminating fragment of) DCC.

So we see that DCC is not just a graded monadic calculus. The rule
Prot-Already
makes it something more than that. This rule enables one to flip the modal type constructors. In DCC, from
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, one can derive
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{y}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, using rule
Prot-Already
. Such a derivation is not possible in a general monadic calculus.

However, if the calculus is also comonadic in addition to being monadic, such a derivation is possible. From
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, using monadic join, we can get
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, which is same as
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, from which we can get
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, using comonadic fork. So it seems that DCC has some comonadic flavour to it. But is DCC a graded comonadic calculus? In order to address this question, we first need to build the theory of a graded comonadic calculus.

5.
Graded Comonadic Calculus

Soon after
Moggi 1991
showed that computational effects can be understood in terms of monads,
Brookes and Geva 1991
showed that intensional behaviour of programs, for example, the number of steps necessary for reduction, can be understood in terms of comonads. While monads can model how programs affect the environment, comonads can model how the environment affects programs. Comonads, with necessary extra structure, have been used by
Uustalu and Vene 2008
and
Petricek
et al. 2013
, among others, to model various notions of environment-dependent computation like resource usage of programs, computation on streams, etc. Several calculi
(
Ghica and Smith 2014
;
Petricek
et al. 2014
;
Brunel
et al. 2014
)
have been developed to provide a general account of such environment-dependent computation. These calculi are parametrized by semiring-like structures, and are modelled using semiring-graded comonads, possibly including additional structure. In this section, we forgo extra structures and present a graded comonadic calculus that is the dual of the graded monadic calculus presented in Section
3
.

The Graded Comonadic Calculus (GCC), similar to GMC, is parametrized by a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
. In addition to the types and terms of standard
\lambda
-calculus, GCC has a graded comonadic type
D_{m}\>\mathit{A}
and terms related to it, shown below.

\displaystyle\text{types},A,B

\displaystyle::=\ldots\>|\>D_{m}\>\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\text{terms},a,b,f,g

\displaystyle::=\ldots\>|\>\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\>|\>\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}

Now we look at the typing rules and the equational theory of the calculus.

5.1.
Type System and Equational Theory

The typing rules for terms related to the graded comonadic type are presented in Figure
10
.

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}

(Typing rules)

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:D_{1}\>\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:C-Extract}{{{C-Extract}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}:D_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to D_{m}\>\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:C-Fmap}{{{C-Fmap}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>D_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:C-Fork}{{{C-Fork}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}\quad m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:C-Up}{{{C-Up}}}
}{},}

Figure 10
.
Typing rules of GCC (excerpt)

The rules
C-Extract
,
C-Fmap
, and
C-Fork
are generalizations of the corresponding rules for the ungraded comonadic type. The rule
C-Up
, like rule
M-Up
, relaxes the grade on the type. If
\mathcal{M}
is the trivial preordered monoid, then the above rules degenerate to the standard typing rules for comonads. Note that rules
C-Fmap
and
C-Up
are essentially the same as rules
M-Fmap
and
M-Up
respectively whereas rules
C-Extract
and
C-Fork
are like ‘inverses’ of rules
M-Return
and
M-Join
respectively.

The equational theory of the calculus appears in Figure
11
(we omit the
\beta\eta
-rules of standard
\lambda
-calculus). For presenting the rules, we use the shorthand notation:
\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}\triangleq(\mathbf{lift}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{f})\>(\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})
where
\mathit{a}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
and
\mathit{f}:D_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
. Note that
\_\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\_
is a graded-
\mathbf{extend}
operator.

(10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}

(11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x}))

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.(\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g})\>((\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{x})

(12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}

(13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\mathit{a}

(14)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}(\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a})

(15)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{f}\>(\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}},m^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{x}))\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}

(16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{extr}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{1}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a}

(17)

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{x})\>\mathchoice{\kern 4.48613pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 4.48613pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 7.47655pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}{\kern 8.94144pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{1}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}

(18)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt\mathit{g}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}(\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathchoice{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 11.49895pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 14.11668pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 15.58157pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{x}))\>\mathchoice{\kern 24.8729pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 24.8729pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 26.45134pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}{\kern 27.91623pt\mathop{}\mathopen{\vphantom{\ll}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}}\ll\!\!=^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}

Figure 11
.
Equality rules of GCC (excerpt)

The first four rules are same as their counterparts in GMC. The next two rules correspond to commutativity of
\mathbf{extend}
with
\mathbf{up}
. The two rules after that correspond to
\mathbf{extr}
being the left and the right identity of
\mathbf{extend}
. The last rule corresponds to associativity of
\mathbf{extend}
.

Next, we want to interpret the calculus in a suitable category. Similar to GMC, the types of standard
\lambda
-calculus can be interpreted in any bicartesian closed category. To interpret the graded comonadic type, we need a strong graded comonad, the dual of a strong graded monad we saw earlier. We briefly go over the definition of a strong graded comonad and present the categorical model thereafter.

5.2.
Graded Comonad and Categorical Model

While a graded monad is a kind of lax monoidal functor, a graded comonad is a kind of oplax monoidal functor. An oplax monoidal functor from a monoidal category
(M,\otimes_{M},1_{M})
to a monoidal category
(N,\otimes_{N},1_{N})
is nothing but a lax monoidal functor from
(M^{\text{op}},\otimes_{M},1_{M})
to
(N^{\text{op}},\otimes_{N},1_{N})
. Now, given a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
, an
\mathcal{M}
-graded comonad over a category
\mathds{C}
is an oplax monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{M})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}
. In order to interpret GCC, we need to add strength to the graded comonad. We follow the same strategy as before and define a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded comonad over a cartesian category
\mathds{C}
as an oplax monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
. We use a strong graded comonad to build the categorical model of GCC.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\_\cdot\_,1,\leq)
be the preordered monoid parametrizing the calculus. Let
\mathds{C}
be any bicartesian closed category. Let
(\mathbf{D},\delta,\epsilon)
be a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded comonad over
\mathds{C}
. Then, the interpretation,
\llbracket\_\rrbracket
, of types and terms of GCC is as given in Figure
12
.

\displaystyle\llbracket D_{m}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\mathbf{D}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{D}_{m}})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\delta^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

Figure 12
.
Interpretation of GCC (excerpt)

Note that
\epsilon
is a natural transformation from
\mathbf{D}_{1}
to
Id
and
\delta^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are morphisms from
\mathbf{D}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
to
\mathbf{D}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\circ\mathbf{D}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, natural in both
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
m_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. By reasoning along the lines of Theorem
3.1
, we can show that the above interpretation provides a sound model for GCC.

Theorem 5.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GCC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GCC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Now that we have a graded comonadic calculus with us, we can test the comonadic character of DCC. In the next section, we explore the relation between DCC and GCC. In particular, we ask: with appropriate restrictions, can we translate GCC into DCC?

6.
DCC and GCC

In Section
4.4
, we saw that DCC is not just a graded monadic calculus. The rule
Prot-Already
lends a comonadic character to it. But does it make DCC a graded comonadic calculus? In other words, over the class of bounded join-semilattices, can we translate GCC into DCC? We can translate
D_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
to
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
. The constructs
\mathbf{lift}
and
\mathbf{up}
can be translated as in Figure
9
. But to translate
\mathbf{extr}
and
\mathbf{fork}
, we need to relook at the protection judgement.

6.1.
Protection Judgement, Revisited

To translate
\mathbf{extr}
and
\mathbf{fork}
, we need to be able to construct functions having types
\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
respectively, for an arbitrary type
\mathit{A}
. However, given the formulation of DCC by
Abadi
et al. 1999
, such a construction is not possible. This is so because in order to construct a function having type
\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
, we need to show that:
\bot\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, for an arbitrary
\mathit{A}
. The protection rules do not allow such a derivation. Similarly, in order to construct a function having type
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, we need to show that:
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, for an arbitrary
\mathit{A}
. Again, such a derivation is not allowed by the protection rules.

However, from a dependency perspective,
\bot\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
are sound judgements. The judgement
\bot\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
is sound because:
\bot
is the lowest security level and as such, the terms of any type are at least as secure as
\bot
. The judgement
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
is sound because:
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
is at least as secure as
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
is also at least as secure as
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, so it must be at least as secure as
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. This reasoning is supported by the lattice model of
Denning 1976
.

Now the above judgements are not only sound, but also desirable. Compared to
\mathit{A}
, the type
\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\>\mathit{A}
offers no extra protection. So, it makes sense to allow programs like the one shown below.

\mathit{x}:\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\>\mathit{A}\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\bot}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y}:\mathit{A}

Next, the type
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
offers no less protection than the type
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
. So, programs like:

\mathit{x}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{y}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}

should be allowed.

Allowing programs like the one above has some interesting consequences. For example, consider the lattice shown below.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{{\mathrm{21}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{{\mathrm{22}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

Here,
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}=\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
. On specializing the example program, we have:

\mathit{x}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\>\mathit{A}\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\mathit{y}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\>\mathit{A}
.

This program shows that we can observe
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
-level values in an environment simultaneously protected by
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
. Two points are worth noting here.

•

First,
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
together offer much much more protection than either of them individually. Observe that neither
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
nor
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
individually offer as much protection as either
\ell_{{\mathrm{21}}}
or
\ell_{{\mathrm{22}}}
. But,
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
together offer more protection than both
\ell_{{\mathrm{21}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{22}}}
. Behind this observation, lies a fundamental security principle, the principle that forms the basis of applications like two-factor authentication, two-man rule, etc. It may be phrased in terms of the age-old proverb: the whole is more than just the sum of its parts.

•

Second,
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
is compromised if
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
are simultaneously compromised. This is so because with a simultaneous access to
\ell_{{\mathrm{11}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{12}}}
, one has access to
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
and all levels below it, even
\ell_{{\mathrm{21}}}
and
\ell_{{\mathrm{22}}}
. It may look counter-intuitive but that just shows the power of simultaneous access and simultaneous protection.

To enable such reasoning within the calculus, we add the following rules to the protection judgement of DCC.

\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}

(Extended Protection Rules)

\frac{}{\bot\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Minimum}{{{Prot-Minimum}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\quad\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\quad\ell\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Prot-Combine}{{{Prot-Combine}}}
}{},}

As a side note, we shall point out that in the categorical model of DCC given by
Abadi
et al. 1999
, for an arbitrary
\mathit{A}
, the interpretations of
\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\>\mathit{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
are the same as those of
\mathit{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
respectively. So, DCC extended with the above rules enjoys the same categorical model. For the sake of precision, we shall call DCC extended with these rules
\text{DCC}_{e}
. The equational theory of
\text{DCC}_{e}
is defined in the same way as that of DCC. So then, DCC is a proper sub-language of
\text{DCC}_{e}
. Since DCC is graded monadic, so is
\text{DCC}_{e}
. Owing to the reasons described above, DCC is not graded comonadic. But
\text{DCC}_{e}
is graded comonadic, as we see next.

6.2.
\text{DCC}_{e}
is a Graded Comonadic Calculus

Over the class of bounded join-semilattices, we can translate GCC into
\text{DCC}_{e}
. Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\bot)
be a bounded join-semilattice. Then, the translation
\overline{\phantom{a}}
is given in Figure
13
. Note the role played by rules
Prot-Minimum
and
Prot-Combine
in the translation of
\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}
and
\mathbf{fork}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}
respectively. The next theorem shows that this translation preserves typing and meaning.

\displaystyle\overline{D_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\overline{\mathit{A}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}^{\bot}\>\mathit{x}=\overline{\mathit{a}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{x}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{lift}^{\ell}\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\overline{\mathit{A}}.\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}(\overline{\mathit{f}}\>\mathit{y})\hskip 3.0pt[\text{Here, }\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}]

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{fork}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{x}=\overline{\mathit{a}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{x}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{up}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{bind}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{x}=\overline{\mathit{a}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{eta}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{x}

Figure 13
.
Translation function from GCC to
\text{DCC}_{e}
(excerpt)

Theorem 6.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\Gamma}\vdash\overline{\mathit{a}}:\overline{\mathit{A}}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\simeq\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
).

Earlier, we showed that DCC is a graded monadic calculus by translating GMC into it. However, we couldn’t translate DCC into GMC because DCC has some comonadic character to it. Thereafter, we designed a graded comonadic calculus, GCC. Using GCC, we put the comonadic character of DCC to test. We could not translate GCC to DCC, thus showing that DCC is not fully comonadic. Next, we found that such a translation is not possible only because DCC does not allow certain derivations that are both sound and desirable. We extended DCC to
\text{DCC}_{e}
to allow these derivations and found that we can translate GCC (and GMC) into
\text{DCC}_{e}
. Now, can we go the other way around and translate
\text{DCC}_{e}
into a calculus built up using just GMC and GCC?

7.
Graded Monadic Comonadic Calculus

7.1.
The Calculus

The Graded Monadic Comonadic Calculus (GMCC) combines the Graded Monadic Calculus (GMC) and the Graded Comonadic Calculus (GCC) into a single system. We can view it as an extension of the standard simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with a graded type constructor
S_{m}
, which behaves both like a graded monadic type constructor,
T_{m}
, and a graded comonadic type constructor,
D_{m}
. The calculus has as terms the union of those of GMC and GCC. The typing rules of the calculus include the rules of GMC and GCC (shown in Figures
2
and
10
respectively) with
S_{m}
replacing
T_{m}
and
D_{m}
.

The equational theory of the calculus is generated by the equational theories of GMC and GCC (presented in Figures
3
and
11
respectively) along with the following additional rules:
\mathbf{extr}\>(\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a})\equiv\mathit{a}
and
\mathbf{ret}\>(\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a})\equiv\mathit{a}
and
\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})\equiv\mathit{a}
and
\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{fork}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a})\equiv\mathit{a}
. These additional rules ensure that the monadic and the comonadic fragments of the calculus behave well with respect to one another.

7.2.
Categorical Model

GMCC enjoys a nice categorical model, as we show next.

We interpret the graded type constructor of the calculus as a kind of strong monoidal functor
(
MacLane 1971
)
. A strong monoidal functor from a monoidal category
(M,\otimes_{M},1_{M})
to a monoidal category
(N,\otimes_{N},1_{N})
is a lax monoidal functor
(F,F_{2},F_{0}):(M,\otimes_{M},1_{M})\to(N,\otimes_{N},1_{N})
where
F_{0}
and
F_{2}(X,Y)
are invertible for all
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(M)
. Thus, for a strong monoidal functor
S:(M,\otimes_{M},1_{M})\to(N,\otimes_{N},1_{N})
, we have:
S(1_{M})\cong 1_{N}
and
S(X\otimes_{M}Y)\cong S(X)\otimes_{N}S(Y)
for all
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(M)
. Note that if these isomorphisms are identities, then the functor is said to be strict. Further, note that the word ‘strong’ in ‘strong monoidal functor’ and in ‘strong endofunctor’ refer to different properties.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\_\cdot\_,1,\leq)
be the preordered monoid parametrizing the calculus. Let
\mathds{C}
