be any bicartesian closed category. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
. Then
\mathbf{S}
is both a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad over
\mathds{C}
and a strong
\mathcal{M}
-graded comonad over
\mathds{C}
. With regard to
\mathbf{S}
, let
\mu,\eta,\delta,\epsilon
denote the corresponding natural transformations. Then,

\displaystyle\epsilon\circ\eta

\displaystyle=\text{id}

\displaystyle\eta\circ\epsilon

\displaystyle=\text{id}

\displaystyle\delta\circ\mu

\displaystyle=\text{id}

\displaystyle\mu\circ\delta

\displaystyle=\text{id}

We interpret
S_{m}\>\mathit{A}
as:
\llbracket S_{m}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\mathbf{S}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
. The terms are interpreted as in Figures
7
and
12
.

This gives us a sound interpretation of the calculus.

Theorem 7.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMCC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMCC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

The theorem above shows that given a preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
, any bicartesian closed category
\mathds{C}
together with a strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}({\mathcal{M}})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{s}
provides a sound model for GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
). In addition to soundness, GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
) also enjoys completeness with respect to its class of categorical models. Formally, we can show:

Theorem 7.2.

Given any preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
, for typing derivations
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
), if
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket
in all models of GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
), then
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
is derivable in GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
).

We use entirely standard term-model techniques
(
Jacobs 1999
)
to prove completeness. First, we construct the classifying category and thereafter, the generic model in the classifying category. The generic model equates only the terms that are equal in the calculus. So, if the interpretations of two GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
)-terms are equal in all models (and therefore in the generic model too), then these terms are equal in the calculus as well. Further, we can also show that:

Theorem 7.3.

The generic model satisfies the universal property.

The above theorem implies that any model of GMCC(
\mathcal{M}
) can be factored through the generic model. We don’t explore the consequences of this theorem here, but leave it for future work.

In this section, we have seen that GMCC is sound and complete with respect to its class of categorical models. In the next section, we explore the relation between GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
.

8.
GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}

We saw that over the class of bounded join-semilattices, we can translate both GMC and GCC into
\text{DCC}_{e}
. In fact, over the same class of structures, we can go further and translate GMCC into
\text{DCC}_{e}
following the translations presented in Figures
9
and
13
.

Theorem 8.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\Gamma}\vdash\overline{\mathit{a}}:\overline{\mathit{A}}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\simeq\overline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
).

We now go the other way around and translate
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
) into GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
).

Note here that
\text{DCC}_{e}
has a very liberal definition of equality, inherited from DCC. Two
\text{DCC}_{e}
terms are equal if, after erasure, they are equal as
\lambda
-terms. So
\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{a}\simeq\mathit{a}
for any
\text{DCC}_{e}
-term
\mathit{a}
. The same is not true in general in GMCC. For example,
\mathbf{up}^{\bot,\ell}(\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a})
may not be equal to
\mathit{a}
. To capture the notion of
\text{DCC}_{e}
-equality in GMCC, we need to define a similar relation between GMCC terms. For GMCC terms
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, we say
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\simeq\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
if and only if
\lfloor\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rfloor
and
\lfloor\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rfloor
, the plain
\lambda
-term counterparts of
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
respectively, are equal as
\lambda
-terms. The erasure operation
\lfloor-\rfloor
strips away the constructors
\mathbf{ret},\mathbf{join},\mathbf{extr},\mathbf{fork},\mathbf{lift}
and
\mathbf{up}
along with the grade annotations. For example,
\lfloor\mathbf{join}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rfloor=\lfloor\mathit{a}\rfloor
.

Coming back to the translation from
\text{DCC}_{e}
to GMCC, it is straightforward for types. The constructor
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
gets translated to
S_{\ell}
. The translation for terms requires the following lemma. We use
\underline{\phantom{a}}
to denote the translation function.

Lemma 8.2.

If
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
, then there exists a term
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{j}:S_{\ell}\>\underline{\mathit{B}}\to\underline{\mathit{B}}
such that
\lfloor\mathit{j}\rfloor\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}
.

The above lemma is key to the translation. Note that in Section
4.4
, while trying to translate DCC to GMC, we could not prove a lemma like this one. Now, with the above lemma, we can translate
\text{DCC}_{e}
terms to GMCC terms, as shown below. The function
\mathit{j}
used in the translation is as given by Lemma
8.2
.

\underline{\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{a}}=\mathbf{up}^{\bot,\ell}(\mathbf{ret}\>\underline{\mathit{a}})\hskip 30.0pt\underline{\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}}=\mathit{j}\>((\mathbf{lift}^{\ell}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\underline{\mathit{b}}))\>\underline{\mathit{a}})

This translation preserves typing and meaning.

Theorem 8.3.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\underline{\Gamma}\vdash\underline{\mathit{a}}:\underline{\mathit{A}}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\simeq\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\underline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\simeq\underline{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
).

Theorems
8.3
and
8.1
together show that over the class of bounded join-semilattices, GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
, seen as dependency calculi, are equivalent. Thus, GMCC is a generalization of the Dependency Core Calculus, DCC. Hence, dependency analysis, at least to the extent DCC is capable of, can be done using just a graded monadic comonadic calculus. This connection of dependency analysis to GMCC is important because:

•

Dependency analysis can now benefit from a wider variety of categorical models. Some of these models may provide simpler proofs of correctness. In fact, using our categorical models, we show, in a straightforward manner, that dependency analyses in
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\lambda^{\circ}
are correct.

•

More dependency calculi can now be unified under a common framework. As a proof of concept, we show that the binding-time analysing calculus
\lambda^{\circ}
of
Davies 2017
can be encoded into
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, an extension of GMCC. Note that
\lambda^{\circ}
can not be translated into DCC
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
)
.

•

The non-standard
bind
-rule of DCC can be replaced with standard monadic and comonadic typing rules. This finding provides insight into the categorical basis of the
bind
-rule of DCC. (See Section
9.1
)

•

GMCC is formed combining GMC and its dual, GCC. GMC can be seen as a restriction of the Explicit Subeffecting Calculus of
Katsumata 2014
. The Explicit Subeffecting Calculus is a general system for analysing effects. This clean connection between dependency analysis and effect analysis promises to be a fertile ground for new ideas, especially in the intersection of dependency, effect and coeffect analyses.

Before closing this section, we want to make some remarks on the equivalence between GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
. From theorems
8.3
and
8.1
, we see that GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
are equivalent upto erasure. We may ask: can this equivalence be made stronger? The answer is yes. In the next section, we shall show that (over the class of bounded join-semilattices) GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
are semantically equivalent too. We can also show something stronger on the syntactic side as well:

Theorem 8.4.

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
be any derivation in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
). Then,
\underline{\overline{\mathit{a}}}\equiv\mathit{a}
.

The above theorem says that any GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
)-term, after a round trip to
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), is equal (not just equal upto erasure) to itself. When going the other way, we can prove the following weaker result: if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
is a derivation in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\overline{\underline{\mathit{a}}}\simeq a
. Note that we are forced to use equality upto erasure here because we don’t have an alternative equational theory for
\text{DCC}_{e}
.

Next, we develop the categorical semantics for
\text{DCC}_{e}
.

9.
\text{DCC}_{e}
: Categorical Semantics

Abadi
et al. 1999
provide a categorical model for DCC and prove noninterference using that model. Several other authors
(
Tse and Zdancewic 2004
;
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
;
Bowman and Ahmed 2015
;
Algehed and
Bernardy 2019
)
have provided alternative proofs of noninterference for DCC using various techniques, including parametricity. In this section, we present a class of categorical models for
\text{DCC}_{e}
, in the style of GMCC, and show noninterference for the calculus using these models. We also establish semantic equivalence between
\text{DCC}_{e}
and GMCC and explain the non-standard
bind
-rule of DCC in terms of standard category-theoretic concepts.

9.1.
Categorical Models for
\text{DCC}_{e}

Given that GMCC and
\text{DCC}_{e}
, considered as dependency calculi, are equivalent, we can simply use models of GMCC to interpret
\text{DCC}_{e}
. However, in this section, we build models for
\text{DCC}_{e}
from first principles and show them to be computationally adequate with respect to a call-by-value semantics. The original operational semantics of DCC, presented by
Abadi
et al. 1999
, is somewhat ad hoc from a categorical perspective because according to this semantics,
\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{a}
converts to
\mathit{a}
. If we interpret
\mathbf{eta}
as the unit of a monad, this conversion would require us to interpret the unit as the identity natural transformation, something that is not very general. On the other hand, a call-by-value semantics or a call-by-name semantics of the calculus
(
Tse and Zdancewic 2004
)
is quite general from a category theoretic perspective. According to a call-by-value semantics,
\mathbf{bind}
-expressions convert in the following manner:

\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}

(Operational Semantics)

\frac{\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}}{\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\leadsto\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a^{\prime}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:CBV-BindLeft}{{{CBV-BindLeft}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{v}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\leadsto\mathit{b}\{\mathit{v}/\mathit{x}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:CBV-BindBeta}{{{CBV-BindBeta}}}
}{},}

Given
\leadsto
, we can define the multistep reduction relation,
\leadsto^{\ast}
, in the usual way. A point to note here is that though we use a call-by-value semantics for
\text{DCC}_{e}
, we could have used a call-by-name semantics as well.
\text{DCC}_{e}
being a terminating calculus, the choice of one evaluation strategy over the other does not lead to significant differences in metatheory. We chose call-by-value over call-by-name because the former allows reductions underneath the
\mathbf{eta}
s.

Given a bounded join-semilattice,
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\bot)
, and any bicartesian closed category
\mathds{C}
, we interpret the graded type constructor
\mathcal{T}
of
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
) as a strong monoidal functor
\mathbf{S}
from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{L})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{s}}
. Formally,
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
. Note that since
\vee
is idempotent, the triple
(\mathbf{S}_{\ell},\mathbf{S}^{\bot\sqsubseteq\ell}\circ\eta,\mu^{\ell,\ell})
, for any
\ell\in L
, is a monad. Further, since
\mu^{\ell,\ell}
is invertible, such a monad is also idempotent.

For interpreting terms, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 9.1.

If
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, then
\exists
an isomorphism
k:\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Further,
k\circ\overline{\eta}=id_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
and
\overline{\eta}\circ k=\text{id}_{\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
where
\overline{\eta}\triangleq\mathbf{S}^{\bot\sqsubseteq\ell}\circ\eta:\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\to\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Using the above lemma, we interpret terms as:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{eta}^{\ell}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}^{\bot\sqsubseteq\ell}\circ\eta\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{bind}^{\ell}\>\mathit{x}=\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=k\circ\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket\circ\text{t}^{\mathbf{S}_{\ell}}\circ\langle\text{id},\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\rangle

For later reference note that whenever we need to be precise, we use
\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}
to refer to the interpretation of
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
) in category
\mathds{C}
using
\mathbf{S}
. The above interpretation of
\text{DCC}_{e}
is sound, as we see next.

Theorem 9.2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Theorem 9.3.

If
\Gamma\vdash a:A
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
, then
\llbracket a\rrbracket=\llbracket a\textquoteright\rrbracket
.

Computational adequacy with respect to call-by-value operational semantics follows as a corollary of the above theorem. Below, we assume that the categorical interpretation is injective for ground types. In particular,
\llbracket\mathbf{true}\rrbracket\neq\llbracket\mathbf{false}\rrbracket
, where
\mathbf{true}\triangleq\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathbf{unit}:\mathbf{Bool}
and
\mathbf{false}\triangleq\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathbf{unit}:\mathbf{Bool}
, where
\mathbf{Bool}\triangleq\mathbf{Unit}+\mathbf{Unit}
.

Theorem 9.4.

Let the interpretation
\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}
be injective for ground types.

•

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathbf{Bool}
and
\mathit{v}
be a value of type
\mathbf{Bool}
. If
\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}=\llbracket\mathit{v}\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}
, then
\vdash\mathit{b}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
.

•

Fix some
\ell\in L
. Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathbf{Bool}
and
\mathit{v}
be a value of type
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathbf{Bool}
. Suppose, the morphisms
\overline{\eta}_{X}\triangleq\mathbf{S}^{\bot\sqsubseteq\ell}_{\mathit{X}}\circ\eta_{\mathit{X}}
are mono for any
X\in\text{Obj}(\mathds{C})
. Now, if
\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}=\llbracket\mathit{v}\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}
, then
\vdash\mathit{b}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
.

Now that GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
) and
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
) enjoy the same class of models, we can show the two calculi are exactly equivalent over these models.

Theorem 9.5.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a bounded join-semilattice.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\llbracket\overline{\mathit{a}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket
.

•

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\llbracket\underline{\mathit{a}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket
.

The categorical semantics of
\text{DCC}_{e}
help in understanding the non-standard
bind
-rule of DCC. By lemma
9.1
, if
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, then
\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\cong\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
. In fact, if
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
is the carrier of an
\mathbf{S}_{\ell}
-algebra. What this means is that the protection judgement is a syntactic mechanism for picking out the appropriate monad algebras. This insight explains the signature of rule
DCC-Bind
:
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}\to(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})\to\{\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}\}\to B
. If
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket
is the carrier of an
\mathbf{S}_{\ell}
-algebra. As such, the return type of the rule can be
\mathit{B}
, in lieu of
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\>\mathit{B}
. The following theorem characterizes the protection judgement in terms of monad algebras.

Theorem 9.6.

If
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
in
\text{DCC}_{e}
, then
(\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket,k)
is an
\mathbf{S}_{\ell}
-algebra.

Further, if
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
and
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{B}
, then for any
f\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket)
,
f
is an
\mathbf{S}_{\ell}
-algebra morphism.

Hence, the full subcategory of
\mathds{C}{}
with
\text{Obj}:=\{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\>|\>\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}\}
is also a full subcategory of the Eilenberg-Moore category,
\mathds{C}{}^{\mathbf{S}_{\ell}}
.

Next, we use these models to prove noninterference for
\text{DCC}_{e}
.

9.2.
Proof of Noninterference

Two functors in
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{s}}
are crucial to our proof of non-interference. One of them is the identity functor,
Id
. The other is the terminal functor, denoted by
\ast
, the functor which maps all objects to
\top
, the terminal object of the category and all morphisms to
\langle\rangle
. Now, for every
\ell\in L
, we define a strong monoidal functor
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{L})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{s}}{}
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{\ell}(\ell^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\textbf{Id},&\text{ if }\ell^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\ell\\
\ast,&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}\mathbf{S}^{\ell}(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\text{id},&\text{ if }\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq\ell\\
\langle\rangle,&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

The following points are worth noting here.

•

\mathbf{S}^{\ell}(\bot)=\textbf{Id}
for any
\ell\in L
. Then, for every
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
,
\eta=\epsilon=\text{id}
.

•

Fix some
\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}\in L
. Now, for any
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\in L
, we have,
\mathbf{S}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}})\circ\mathbf{S}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}(\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})=\mathbf{S}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
. Then,
\mu^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=\delta^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=\text{id}
. Therefore, the
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
s are all strict monoidal functors.

Now, for any bicartesian closed category
\mathds{C}
, any strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{L})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{s}}{}
provides a computationally adequate interpretation of
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
), provided the interpretation for ground types is injective. As such,

Theorem 9.7.

\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{S}^{\ell})}
, for any
\ell\in L
, is a computationally adequate interpretation of
\text{DCC}_{e}
(
\mathcal{L}
).

Next, we explain the intuition behind these strong monoidal functors.
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
keeps untouched all information at levels
\ell^{\prime}
where
\ell^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\ell
but blacks out all information at every other level. So,
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
corresponds to the view of an observer at level
\ell
. We can formalize this intuition. Suppose
\neg(\ell^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\ell)
, i.e.
\ell
should not depend upon
\ell^{\prime\prime}
. Then, if
\ell^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
, the terms of type
A
should not be visible to an observer at level
\ell
. In other words, if
\ell^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
,
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
should black out all information from type
A
. This is indeed the case, as we see next.

Lemma 9.8.

If
\ell^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
and
\neg(\ell^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq\ell)
, then
\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S}^{\ell})}\cong\top
.

The above lemma takes us to our noninterference theorem. Recall the test of correctness for DCC from Section
4.1
: if
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
and
\neg(\ell\sqsubseteq\ell^{\prime})
, then the terms of
\mathit{A}
are not be visible at
\ell^{\prime}
. We prove correctness for
\text{DCC}_{e}
by formulating this test as:

Theorem 9.9.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\bot)
be the parametrizing semilattice.

•

Suppose
\ell\in L
such that
\neg(\ell\sqsubseteq\bot)
. Let
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
. Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathbf{Bool}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
. Then,
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
if and only if
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
, where
\mathit{v}
is a value of type
\mathbf{Bool}
.

•

Suppose
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in L
such that
\neg(\ell\sqsubseteq\ell^{\prime})
. Let
\ell\sqsubseteq\mathit{A}
. Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathcal{T}_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
. Then,
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
if and only if
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\leadsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
, where
\mathit{v}
is a value of type
\mathcal{T}_{\ell^{\prime}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
.

As corollary of the above theorem, we can show that
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{Secret}}\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathbf{Bool}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f^{\prime}}:(\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{Secret}}\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\to\mathbf{Bool}
, for all types
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, are constant functions. We can also show that
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f^{\prime\prime}}:\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
, for any type
\mathit{A}
, is a constant function, whenever
\neg(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
.

We use the
\mathbf{S}^{\ell}
s to prove the noninterference theorem above. This technique relies on the observation that for two entities
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, if
E_{1}
can be present when
E_{2}
is absent, then
E_{1}

does not depend
upon
E_{2}
. We call this technique the
presence-absence test
. In the next section, we shall use the same technique to prove correctness of binding-time analysis in
\lambda^{\circ}
.

10.
Binding-Time Calculus,
\lambda^{\circ}

\lambda^{\circ}

(
Davies 2017
)
is a foundational calculus for binding-time analysis, lying at the heart of state-of-the-art metaprogramming languages like MetaOCaml
(
Calcagno
et al. 2003
)
.
\lambda^{\circ}
is essentially a dependency calculus that ensures early stage computations do not depend upon later stage ones. One might expect that DCC, being a core calculus of dependency, would subsume
\lambda^{\circ}
. However,
Abadi
et al. 1999
noted that
\lambda^{\circ}
cannot be translated into DCC. One reason behind this shortcoming is that DCC does not fully utilize the power of comonadic aspect of dependency analysis, as we discussed before. We extended DCC to
\text{DCC}_{e}
to include the comonadic aspect of dependency analysis. This extension opens up the possibility of
\lambda^{\circ}
being translated to
\text{DCC}_{e}
. In this section, we explore this possibility. We first review the calculus
\lambda^{\circ}
, thereafter present a categorical model leading to an alternative proof of correctness and finally show how we can translate
\lambda^{\circ}
into our graded monadic comonadic system.

10.1.
The Calculus
\lambda^{\circ}

\lambda^{\circ}
is simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus extended with a ‘next time’ type constructor,
\bigcirc
. Intuitively,
\bigcirc\mathit{A}
is the type of terms to be computed upon the ‘next time’. The calculus models staged computation, with an earlier stage manipulating programs from later stage as data. For a time-ordered normalization, the calculus needs to ensure that computation from an earlier stage
does not depend
upon computation from a later stage. To model such a notion of independence of the past from the future,
Davies 2017
uses temporal logic. In
\lambda^{\circ}
, time is discretized as instants or moments, represented by natural numbers. For example,
0
denotes the present moment,
0^{\prime}
denotes the next moment and so on. We now look at the calculus formally, as presented by
Davies 2017
.

The grammar of
\lambda^{\circ}
appears in Figure
14
, typing rules in Figure
15
and the equational theory in Figure
16
. The typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}
intuitively means that
\mathit{a}
is available at time instant
n
, provided the variables in
\Gamma
are available at their respective time instants. Note that
\lambda^{\circ}
does not have sum types; we include them here for the sake of having non-trivial ground types.

\displaystyle\text{Types},\mathit{A},\mathit{B}

\displaystyle::=K\>|\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\>|\>\bigcirc\mathit{A}\>|\>\mathbf{Unit}\>|\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\text{Terms},\mathit{a},\mathit{b}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{x}\>|\>\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\>|\>\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{unit}\>|\>\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\text{Contexts},\Gamma

\displaystyle::=\emptyset\>|\>\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A}

Figure 14
.
Grammar of
\lambda^{\circ}

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}

(Typing)

\frac{}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Var}{{{LC-Var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{n}\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{n}\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Lam}{{{LC-Lam}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:^{n}\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-App}{{{LC-App}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Inj1}{{{LC-Inj1}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{n}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Case}{{{LC-Case}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n+0^{\prime}}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a}:^{n}\bigcirc\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Next}{{{LC-Next}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\bigcirc\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a}:^{n+0^{\prime}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:LC-Prev}{{{LC-Prev}}}
}{},}

.

\lambda^{\circ}

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b})\>\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{b}\{\mathit{a}/\mathit{x}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{b}

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\>\mathit{x}

\displaystyle\mathbf{prev}\>(\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{next}\>(\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a})

\displaystyle\mathbf{case}\>(\mathbf{inj}_{i}\>a_{i})\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\lambda\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b}\>(\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\>;\>\lambda\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b}\>(\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})

.
Equality rules of
\lambda^{\circ}
(Excerpt)

With this background on
\lambda^{\circ}
, let us now build categorical models for the calculus.

10.2.
Categorical Models for
\lambda^{\circ}

The motivation for categorical models of
\lambda^{\circ}
, in the style of GMCC, comes from the observation that rules
LC-Next
and
LC-Prev
are like rules
C-Fork
and
M-Join
respectively. Here, we can think of
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},+,0)
with discrete ordering to be the parametrizing preordered monoid. Then,
\bigcirc
is like
S_{0^{\prime}}
, where
S
is the graded modal type constructor.
\lambda^{\circ}
and GMCC(
\mathcal{N}
) share several similarities, but there is a crucial difference between the two calculi. In
\lambda^{\circ}
, the types
\bigcirc^{n}(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})
and
\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{A}\to\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{B}
(where
\bigcirc^{n}
is the operator
\bigcirc
applied
n
times) are isomorphic whereas in GMCC(
\mathcal{N}
), the types
S_{n}\>(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})
and
S_{n}\>\mathit{A}\to S_{n}\>\mathit{B}
are not necessarily isomorphic. Owing to this difference, we need to modify our models in order to interpret
\lambda^{\circ}
. More precisely, unlike
S_{n}
, we cannot model
\bigcirc
using any strong endofunctor, but require cartesian closed endofunctors. So next, we define the category of cartesian closed endofunctors.

Let
\mathds{C}
be a cartesian closed category. An endofunctor
F:\mathds{C}\to\mathds{C}
is said to be finite-product-preserving if and only if the morphisms
p_{\top}\triangleq\langle\rangle:F(\top)\to\top
and
p_{X,Y}\triangleq\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle:F(X\times Y)\to FX\times FY
, for
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(\mathds{C})
, have inverses. A finite-product-preserving endofunctor
F:\mathds{C}\to\mathds{C}
is said to be cartesian closed if and only if the morphisms
q_{X,Y}\triangleq\Lambda(F(\text{app})\circ p^{-1}_{Y^{X},X}):F(Y^{X})\to(FY)^{(FX)}
, for
X,Y\in\text{Obj}(\mathds{C})
, have inverses. The cartesian closed endofunctors of
\mathds{C}
, with natural transformations as morphisms, form a category,
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}
. Like
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{s}}{}
,
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}{}
is also a strict monoidal category with the monoidal product and the identity object defined in the same way. We use
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}{}
to build models for
\lambda^{\circ}
.

Let
\mathds{C}
be any bicartesian closed category. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{N})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}
. Then, the interpretation
\llbracket\_\rrbracket
, or more precisely
\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{(\mathds{C},\mathbf{S})}
, of types and terms is as follows. The modal operator and contexts are interpreted as:

\llbracket\bigcirc\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\mathbf{S}_{0^{\prime}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hskip 20.0pt\llbracket\emptyset\rrbracket=\top\hskip 20.0pt\llbracket\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

Terms are interpreted as:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathit{x}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rrbracket\times\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\times\llbracket\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\Lambda\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket}(\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket)^{(\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}\xrightarrow{q^{-1}}\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\langle q\circ\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\rangle}(\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket)^{(\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}\times\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\text{app}}\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{S}_{n+0^{\prime}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\delta^{n,0^{\prime}}}\mathbf{S}_{n}\mathbf{S}_{0^{\prime}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{S}_{n}\mathbf{S}_{0^{\prime}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mu^{n,0^{\prime}}}\mathbf{S}_{n+0^{\prime}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket}\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mathbf{S}_{n}i_{1}}\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\langle\langle\llbracket\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\rangle,\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\rangle}(\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket}\times\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket})\times\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;\,\xrightarrow{p^{-1}\times\text{id}}\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket}\times\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket})\times\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;\,\xrightarrow{p^{-1}}\mathbf{S}_{n}((\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket}\times\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket})\times(\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket))

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;\,\cong\mathbf{S}_{n}(\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket}\times(\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket+\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket))\xrightarrow{\mathbf{S}_{n}\text{app}}\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket

This gives us a sound interpretation of
\lambda^{\circ}
.

Theorem 10.1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}
in
\lambda^{\circ}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in
\lambda^{\circ}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\mathbf{S}_{n}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Such an interpretation is also computationally adequate, provided it is injective for ground types. The operational semantics for the calculus is assumed to be the call-by-value semantics induced by the
\beta
-rules in Figure
16
. We denote the multi-step reduction relation corresponding to this operational semantics by
\longmapsto^{\ast}
. Formally, we can state adequacy as:

Theorem 10.2.

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:^{0}\mathbf{Bool}
and
\mathit{v}
be a value of type
\mathbf{Bool}
. If
\llbracket\mathit{b}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{v}\rrbracket
then
\vdash\mathit{b}\longmapsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
.

Next, we use these categorical models to show correctness of binding-time analysis in
\lambda^{\circ}
.

10.3.
Correctness of Binding-Time Analysis in
\lambda^{\circ}

A binding-time analysis is correct if computation from an earlier stage
does not depend
upon computation from a later stage. Here, using the categorical model, we shall show that
\lambda^{\circ}
satisfies this condition. We shall use the same
presence-absence test
technique that we used to prove noninterference for
\text{DCC}_{e}
. The goal is to show that computations at a given stage can proceed when computations from all later stages are blacked out. Towards this end, we present a strong monoidal functor
\mathbf{S}^{0}
that keeps untouched all computations at time instant
0
but blacks out all computations from every time instant greater than
0
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{0}(n)=\begin{cases}\textbf{Id}&\text{ if }n=0\\
\ast&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Note that
\mathbf{S}^{0}
is, in fact, a strict monoidal functor because
\eta=\epsilon=\text{id}
and
\delta=\mu=\text{id}
. Now, for any bicartesian closed category
\mathds{C}
, any strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{N})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}{}
provides a computationally adequate interpretation of
\lambda^{\circ}
, given the interpretation for ground types is injective. As such,

Theorem 10.3.

\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{S}^{0})}
is a computationally adequate interpretation of
\lambda^{\circ}
.

The existence of
\mathbf{S}^{0}
shows that binding-time analysis in
\lambda^{\circ}
is correct. We elaborate on this point below. By the above theorem, computations at time instant
0
can proceed independently of computations from all later stages. Once the computations from time instant
0
are done, we can move on to computations from time instant
0^{\prime}
, which now acts like the new
0
. Then, using the same argument, we can show that computations at time instant
0^{\prime}
can proceed independently of all later stage computations. Repeating this argument over and over again, we see that we can normalize
\lambda^{\circ}
-expressions in a time-ordered manner. Therefore, binding-time analysis in
\lambda^{\circ}
is correct.

We can formalize the above argument into the following noninterference theorem.

Theorem 10.4.

Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{0}\bigcirc\mathit{A}\to\mathbf{Bool}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{0}\bigcirc\mathit{A}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{0}\bigcirc\mathit{A}
. Then,
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\longmapsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
if and only if
\vdash\mathit{f}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\longmapsto^{\ast}\mathit{v}
, where
\mathit{v}
is a value of type
\mathbf{Bool}
.

The presence-absence test provides a simple yet powerful method for proving correctness of dependency analyses. We used it to show correctness of
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\lambda^{\circ}
in a very straightforward manner. To put it in perspective,
Davies 2017
requires 10 journal pages to establish correctness of
\lambda^{\circ}
using syntactic methods whereas our proof of correctness for
\lambda^{\circ}
follows almost immediately from the soundness theorem. This shows that the presence-absence test may be a useful tool for establishing correctness of dependency calculi.

Next, we show how to translate
\lambda^{\circ}
into a graded monadic comonadic framework.

10.4.
Can We Translate
\lambda^{\circ}
to GMCC?

Here, we consider how we might translate
\lambda^{\circ}
to GMCC. We can instantiate the parametrizing preordered monoid
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},+,0)
and we may translate
\bigcirc
as:
\widehat{\bigcirc\mathit{A}}=S_{0^{\prime}}\>\widehat{\mathit{A}}
. We can translate contexts as:

\widehat{\emptyset}=\emptyset\hskip 20.0pt\widehat{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{n}\mathit{A}}=\widehat{\Gamma},\mathit{x}:S_{n}\>\widehat{\mathit{A}}

A typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}
can then be translated as:
\widehat{\Gamma}\vdash\widehat{\mathit{a}}:S_{n}\>\widehat{\mathit{A}}
. For the modal terms, we have:

\widehat{\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a}}=\mathbf{fork}^{n,0^{\prime}}\widehat{\mathit{a}}\hskip 20.0pt\widehat{\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a}}=\mathbf{join}^{n,0^{\prime}}\widehat{\mathit{a}}

This translation works well for the modal constructs; however, we run into a problem when dealing with functions and applications. The problem is that with the above translation, it is not possible to show typing is preserved in case of functions and applications. The reason behind this problem is the difference between
\lambda^{\circ}
and GMCC we referred to earlier: the types
\bigcirc^{n}(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})
and
\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{A}\to\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{B}
are isomorphic in
\lambda^{\circ}
but not necessarily in GMCC.

This difference arises from the fact that in
\lambda^{\circ}
, the grades pervade all the typing rules, including the ones for functions and applications, while in GMCC, they are restricted to the monadic and the comonadic typing rules. We could have designed GMCC by permeating the grades along all the typing rules in lieu of restricting them to a fragment of the calculus. We avoided such a design for the sake of simplicity. However, now that we understand the calculus, we can consider the implications of such a design choice. In the next section, we explore this design choice and present
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, GMCC extended with graded contexts and graded typing judgements.

11.
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}

11.1.
The Calculus

Like GMCC,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
is parametrized by an arbitrary preordered monoid,
\mathcal{M}
. The types of the calculus are the same as those of GMCC. With respect to terms,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
differs from GMCC in having only the following two term-level constructs (in lieu of
ret
,
extr
, etc.) for introducing and eliminating the modal type.

\text{terms},a,b::=\ldots(\lambda\text{-calculus terms})\>|\>\mathbf{split}^{m}\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{merge}^{m}\mathit{a}

\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
also differs from GMCC wrt contexts and typing judgements, both of which are graded in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, like in
\lambda^{\circ}
. The typing judgement of
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A}
, intuitively means that
\mathit{a}
can be observed at
m
, provided the variables in
\Gamma
are observable at their respective grades. The typing rules of the calculus appear in Figure
17
. The rules pertaining to standard
\lambda
-calculus terms are as expected. The rules
E-Split
and
E-Merge
introduce and eliminate the modal type. These rules are similar to rule
C-Fork
and rule
M-Join
respectively. The rule
E-Up
, akin to rules
M-Up
and
C-Up
, implicitly relaxes the grade at which a term is observed.

The equational theory of the calculus is induced by the
\beta\eta
-rules of standard
\lambda
-calculus along with the following
\beta\eta
-rule for modal terms.

\mathbf{merge}^{m}(\mathbf{split}^{m}\mathit{a})\equiv\mathit{a}\hskip 25.0pt\mathit{a}\equiv\mathbf{split}^{m}(\mathbf{merge}^{m}\mathit{a})

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A}

(Typing)

\frac{}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{m}\mathit{A},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{m}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Var}{{{E-Var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{m}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{m}\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{m}\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Lam}{{{E-Lam}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}:^{m}\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-App}{{{E-App}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Pair}{{{E-Pair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{proj}_{i}\>\mathit{a}:^{m}A_{i}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Proj}{{{E-Proj}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash a_{i}:^{m}A_{i}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{i}\>a_{i}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Inj}{{{E-Inj}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{case}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{m}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Case}{{{E-Case}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{split}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}S_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Split}{{{E-Split}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}S_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{merge}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Merge}{{{E-Merge}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{m}\mathbf{Unit}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Unit}{{{E-Unit}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{A}\quad m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:E-Up}{{{E-Up}}}
}{},}

Figure 17
.
Typing Rules of
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}

The graded presentation of the calculus leads to some interesting consequences. Unlike GMCC, this calculus enjoys the following properties.

Proposition 11.1.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be any preordered monoid. Then, in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{M}
),

•

The types
S_{m}\>\mathbf{Unit}
and
\mathbf{Unit}
are isomorphic.

•

The types
S_{m}\>(\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
and
S_{m}\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times S_{m}\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, for all types
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, are isomorphic.

•

The types
S_{m}\>(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})
and
S_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to S_{m}\>\mathit{B}
, for all types
\mathit{A}
and
\mathit{B}
, are isomorphic.

The third property above reminds us of the isomorphism between types
\bigcirc^{n}(\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B})
and
\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{A}\to\bigcirc^{n}\mathit{B}
in
\lambda^{\circ}
. Recall that we could not translate
\lambda^{\circ}
into GMCC because such an isomorphism does not hold in general in GMCC. We shall see,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, that satisfies these isomorphisms, can readily capture
\lambda^{\circ}
.

Next, we build categorical models for
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
. The models are similar to those of GMCC; however, as in the case of
\lambda^{\circ}
, we need to use cartesian closed endofunctors in lieu of just strong endofunctors. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the parametrizing structure. Let
\mathds{C}
be any bicartesian closed category. Further, let
\mathbf{S}
be a strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{M})
to
\mathbf{End}_{\mathds{C}}^{\text{cc}}
. Then, the interpretation
\llbracket\_\rrbracket
of types, contexts and terms is as follows.

\llbracket S_{m}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\mathbf{S}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hskip 20.0pt\llbracket\emptyset\rrbracket=\top\hskip 20.0pt\llbracket\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{m}\mathit{A}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{S}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{split}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\delta^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbf{merge}^{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mu^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}\mathbf{S}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket

Note that the types and terms of the standard
\lambda
-calculus are interpreted as in the case of
\lambda^{\circ}
.

This gives us a sound interpretation of the calculus.

Theorem 11.2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{m}\mathit{A}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\mathbf{S}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{m}\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\mathbf{S}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Next, we show that both
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\lambda^{\circ}
can be translated to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
.

11.2.
Translations from
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\lambda^{\circ}
to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}

We know that, over the class of bounded join-semilattices,
\text{DCC}_{e}
is equivalent to GMCC. Given that GMCC is quite close to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, we shall use GMCC as the source language for our translation. Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\vee,\bot)
be an arbitrary join-semilattice. Then, the translation
\widetilde{\phantom{a}}
from GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
) to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{L}
) is as follows. For types,
\widetilde{\mathit{A}}=\mathit{A}
. For terms,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{split}^{\bot}\widetilde{\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{extr}\>\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{merge}^{\bot}\widetilde{\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{join}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{split}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{merge}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{merge}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\widetilde{\mathit{a}}))

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{fork}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{split}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}(\mathbf{split}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{merge}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vee\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\widetilde{\mathit{a}}))

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{lift}^{\ell}\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{split}^{\ell}(\widetilde{\mathit{f}}\>(\mathbf{merge}^{\ell}\mathit{x}))

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{up}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{split}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}(\mathbf{merge}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\widetilde{\mathit{a}})

The standard
\lambda
-calculus terms are translated in the expected manner. This translation is sound, as we see next. Below we use
\Gamma^{m}
to denote the graded counterpart of
\Gamma
where every assumption is held at
m
.

Theorem 11.3.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\Gamma^{\bot}\vdash\widetilde{\mathit{a}}:^{\bot}\mathit{A}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{L}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
), then
\widetilde{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\equiv\widetilde{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{L}
).

One might wonder here whether we can go the other way around and translate
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{L}
) to GMCC(
\mathcal{L}
). Though the two calculi are very similar, such a translation is not possible, owing to Proposition
11.1
. More concretely, observe that the type
S_{\mathbf{Secret}}\>(S_{\mathbf{Secret}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool})
has four distinct terms in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) but only two distinct terms in GMCC(
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) (
\mathcal{L}_{2}\triangleq\mathbf{Public}\sqsubset\mathbf{Secret}
).

Next, we return to our incomplete translation of
\lambda^{\circ}
from Section
10.4
. Though we couldn’t translate
\lambda^{\circ}
to GMCC(
\mathcal{N}
), we can now easily translate it to
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{N}
). The translation for the modal types and terms is as follows:

\widehat{\bigcirc\mathit{A}}=S_{0^{\prime}}\>\widehat{\mathit{A}}\hskip 15.0pt\widehat{\mathbf{next}\>\mathit{a}}=\mathbf{split}^{0^{\prime}}\widehat{\mathit{a}}\hskip 15.0pt\widehat{\mathbf{prev}\>\mathit{a}}=\mathbf{merge}^{0^{\prime}}\widehat{\mathit{a}}

This translation is sound, as we see next. Below,
\widehat{\Gamma}
denotes
\Gamma
with the types of the assumptions translated.

Theorem 11.4.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{n}\mathit{A}
in
\lambda^{\circ}
, then
\widehat{\Gamma}\vdash\widehat{\mathit{a}}:^{n}\widehat{\mathit{A}}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{N}
). Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{n}\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in
\lambda^{\circ}
, then
\widehat{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\equiv\widehat{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
(
\mathcal{N}
).

We see that both
\text{DCC}_{e}
and
\lambda^{\circ}
can be soundly translated into
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
. Hence,
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
is more general in its analysis of dependencies.
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
shares some similarities with the sealing calculus
(
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
)
, which also subsumes the terminating fragment of DCC. Akin to
split
and
merge
in
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
, the sealing calculus uses
seal
and
unseal
to introduce and eliminate the modal type. However, the sealing calculus is less general because it works for lattices only whereas
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
works for any preordered monoid.

12.
Discussions and Related Work

12.1.
Nontermination

Till now, we did not include nontermination in any of our calculi. DCC, as presented by
Abadi
et al. 1999
, includes nonterminating computations. So here we discuss how we can add nontermination to GMCC and
\text{GMCC}_{\text{e}}
. One of the features of these calculi is that they can be decomposed into a standard
\lambda
-calculus fragment and a modal fragment. The categorical models for the calculi also reflect the separation between the two fragments. The
\lambda
-calculus fragment is modelled by a bicartesian closed category,
\mathds{C}
, whereas the modal fragment is modelled using monoidal functors from the parametrizing monoid to the category of endofunctors over
\mathds{C}
. This separation between the two fragments makes it easier to add nontermination to these calculi. To include nontermination in these calculi, we just need to add the modal fragment on top of an already nonterminating calculus, for example,
\lambda
-calculus with pointed types. The nonterminating calculus can then be modelled by an appropriate category
\mathds{D}
, for example,
\mathbf{Cpo}
, and the modal fragment by monoidal functors from the parametrizing monoid to the category of endofunctors over
\mathds{D}
. Owing to the separation between the fragments, we conjecture that the proofs of the theorems would carry over smoothly to the new setting.

12.2.
Algehed’s SDCC

Algehed 2018
is similar in spirit to our work.
Algehed 2018
designs a calculus, SDCC, that is equivalent to (the terminating fragment of) DCC. SDCC has the same types as DCC. However, it replaces the
\mathbf{bind}
construct of DCC with four new constructs:
\mu,map,up
and
com
. The constructs
\mu,map
and
up
serve the same purpose as
\mathbf{join},\mathbf{lift}
and
\mathbf{up}
respectively. The construct
com
is what separates SDCC from our work. While
Algehed 2018
went with
com
and designed a calculus equivalent to DCC, we went with
\mathbf{extr}
and
\mathbf{fork}
and designed a calculus that is equivalent to an extension of DCC. This choice helped us realize the power of comonadic aspect of dependency analysis.

12.3.
Relational Semantics of DCC, Revisited

Abadi
et al. 1999
present a relational categorical model for DCC. They interpret each of the
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
s as a separate monad on a base category,
\mathcal{DC}
. However, their interpretation can also be phrased in terms of a strong monoidal functor from
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{L})
to
\mathbf{End}^{s}_{\mathcal{DC}}
. In other words, the model given by
Abadi
et al. 1999
is an instance of the general class of models admitted by
\text{DCC}_{e}
, and hence DCC.

In this regard, we want to point out a technical problem with the category
\mathcal{DC}
. Since
\mathcal{DC}
models simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus, it should be cartesian closed.
Abadi
et al. 1999
claim that it is so. However, contrary to their claim, the category
\mathcal{DC}
is not cartesian closed. The exponential object in
\mathcal{DC}
does not satisfy the universal property, unless the relations in the definition of
\text{Obj}(\mathcal{DC})
are restricted to reflexive ones only. Note that such modified objects are nothing but classified sets that
Kavvos 2019
uses to build a categorical model of DCC based on cohesion. How the cohesion-based models relate to our graded models of DCC is something we would like to explore in future.

13.
Conclusion

Dependency analysis is as much an analysis of dependence as of independence. By controlling the flow of information, it aims to ensure that certain entities are independent of certain other entities while being dependent upon yet other entities. To control flow, it needs to make use of unidirectional devices, devices that allow flow along one direction but block along the other. The two simple yet robust unidirectional devices in programming languages are monads and comonads. Monads allow inflow but block outflow whereas comonads allow outflow but block inflow. When used together, they ensure that flow respects the constraints imposed upon it by a predetermined structure, like a preordered monoid. Such a controlled flow can be used for a variety of purposes like enforcing security constraints, analysing binding-time, etc.

I was supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant Nos. 2006535 and 1703835.

I would like to thank Dominic Orchard, Benjamin Bergman, Stephanie Weirich and the anonymous referees for their feedback and suggestions. I dedicate this paper to Master Felice Macera of the Penn TaeKwonDo Club, who has been a mentor par excellence.

Appendix A
Proofs of lemmas/theorems stated in Section
3

Theorem A.1 (Theorem
3.1
).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
in GMC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
. Further, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
. We show
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
by induction on the typing derivation.

•

\lambda
-calculus. Standard.

•

Rule
M-Return
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}:T_{1}\>\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
.

By IH,
\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Now,
\llbracket\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\eta_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}\mathbf{T}_{1}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{ret}\>\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket T_{1}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

•

Rule
M-Fmap
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
.

Now,
\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\rrbracket=\Lambda\Big(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}}\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)\xrightarrow{\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)}\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\Big)
.

Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{B}\rrbracket)
.

•

Rule
M-Join
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
.

By IH,
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Now,
\llbracket\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mu^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{join}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

•

Rule
M-Up
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
and
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

By IH,
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Now,
\llbracket\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket=\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\mathbf{T}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
.

Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{C}}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket T_{m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\rrbracket)
.

Next, we show that if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}
such that
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in GMC, then
\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket
.

By inversion on
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\equiv\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
:

•

\lambda
-calculus. Standard.

•

\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x})\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}
.

Given:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}):T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{A}
.

Now,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{x})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Lambda\Big(\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\mathbf{T}_{m}\pi_{2}\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket})

\displaystyle=\Lambda\pi_{2}\hskip 2.0pt[\text{By naturality and strength}]

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lambda\mathit{x}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}\rrbracket.

•

\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x}))\equiv\lambda\mathit{x}.(\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g})\>((\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{x})
.

Given:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x})):T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{C}
and
\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}.(\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g})\>((\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{x}):T_{m}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{m}\>\mathit{C}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{g}:\mathit{B}\to\mathit{C}
.

Now,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}(\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x}))\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Lambda\Big(\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{g}\>(\mathit{f}\>\mathit{x})\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Lambda\Big(\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{g}\rrbracket\circ\langle\pi_{1},\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket\rangle)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\Big).

Next,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt\llbracket\lambda\mathit{x}:T_{m}\>\mathit{A}.(\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g})\>((\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f})\>\mathit{x})\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\Lambda\Big(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{g}\rrbracket\circ\langle\pi_{1},\Lambda^{-1}\mathbf{lift}^{m}\mathit{f}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\Lambda\Big(\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{g}\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket}\circ\langle\pi_{1},\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)\circ t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\rangle\Big).

The morphisms above are equal because the squares below commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\times\mathbf{T}_{m}(\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}\langle\pi_{1},\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\times\Lambda^{-1}\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket)}

The right square commutes by naturality whereas the left one commutes because the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket)\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}((\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket)\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times(\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket},\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\rangle\times\mathbf{T}_{m}\text{id}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}(\langle\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket},\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\rangle\times\text{id}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\mathbf{T}_{m}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{m}\alpha^{-1}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\times t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\mathbf{T}_{m}}_{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\times\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}}

Figure 18
.
Commutative diagram

The square above commutes by naturality whereas the rectangle below commutes by strength.

\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}\equiv\mathit{a}
.

Given:
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:T_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{A}
.

Since
\mathbf{T}
is a functor,
\mathbf{T}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}=\text{id}_{\mathbf{T}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}
.

Therefore,
\llbracket\mathbf{up}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{a}\rrbracket=\mathbf{T}^{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}_{\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket}\circ\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{a}\rrbracket
.
